================================================================================
2026-08-22  THE DISPATCH STAGE NOW HAS BRACKETS. Instrument before optimising.
================================================================================

DispatchJobs is the largest single item in the streaming tick -- ~44% of a tick
that is 73-84% of wall clock, i.e. 0.21 ms of GAME THREAD PER DISPATCHED CHUNK
-- and throughput is MaxJobsInFlight x frame rate, so that per-chunk cost IS the
world's generation ceiling. `recompute` has carried exitScanMs/sortMs/entryMs for
months. Dispatch carried ONE number.

Split into three, because they have different fixes:

  airProofMs  IsChunkProvablyAllAir -> FootprintSurfaceUpperBoundMm, which walks
              a dilated cubic-B-spline control grid with Lipschitz differences
              and tile lookups. Worldgen-grade work, once per candidate, and the
              ONLY such call on this path that is not memoised -- its sibling
              twenty lines away, FootprintSolidFloorMmCached, IS. A predicate
              that is too expensive gets memoised.
  bandMs      the footprint-band cache consult. Expected small; measured rather
              than assumed, because "it is only a hash lookup" is exactly the
              kind of claim that turns out to be a hash lookup inside a loop
              that runs 8,500 times per tick.
  submitMs    building and handing off the job -- the fork's region request
              (which now also samples per-chunk shading) or the worker enqueue.
              A submit that is too expensive gets batched or moved off-thread.

Printed as an explicit SUM CHECK:
  "Hitch frame dispatch: dispatchMs=A = airProofMs=B + bandMs=C + submitMs=D
   + other=E"
so a large `other` says the cost is in the loop's own bookkeeping and none of
the three named fixes would touch it. That is a real possible answer and the
line is shaped so it cannot be overlooked.

WHY THIS AND NOT THE CACHE. A subagent's read identified the missing memo on
FootprintSurfaceUpperBoundMm as the highest-value candidate, and it is a good
candidate -- but it is a HYPOTHESIS FROM READING, not a profile. Writing the
cache first would have produced a number that could only be compared against
another number from the same unbracketed bucket. Tonight has already produced
four wrong conclusions from comparing unlike things; adding an instrument before
adding an optimisation is the cheap way not to make it five.

  RESULT, AND IT FALSIFIES THE RECOMMENDED FIX. Leg dispatch-brackets, LINE
  flight, marcher shipping config:

    dispatchMs=21.05 = airProofMs=0.51 + bandMs=0.00 + submitMs=1.56 + other=18.98
    dispatchMs=21.10 = airProofMs=0.36 + bandMs=0.00 + submitMs=2.06 + other=18.68
    dispatchMs=20.92 = airProofMs=0.45 + bandMs=0.00 + submitMs=1.89 + other=18.58
    dispatchMs=26.67 = airProofMs=5.42 + bandMs=0.00 + submitMs=2.04 + other=19.20
    dispatchMs=17.97 = airProofMs=0.00 + bandMs=0.00 + submitMs=0.00 + other=17.97

  IN STEADY STATE THE AIR PROOF IS ~0.4-0.5 ms OF A ~21 ms DISPATCH -- about 2%.
  Memoising FootprintSurfaceUpperBoundMm, the change identified as the
  highest-value candidate, would therefore buy roughly 2% of the largest item in
  the tick. Worth having eventually; not the ceiling.

  `other` IS 18-19 ms, ~88% OF DISPATCH, AND IT IS THE WHOLE PROBLEM. That is
  everything in the loop besides the three named calls: the queue pops, the
  record lookups, the admission and ring-quota checks, the per-candidate
  bookkeeping. It is also remarkably STABLE -- 17.65 to 19.20 across every
  sampled hitch frame, including the one where airProof exploded -- which is the
  signature of a fixed per-candidate cost paid on every iteration rather than of
  anything data-dependent.

  THE AIR PROOF DOES SPIKE, AND THAT IS A SEPARATE FINDING: two frames show
  airProofMs=192.82 and 204.23 against a ~0.4 ms median. So the memo is not
  worthless -- it would flatten a real tail -- it simply is not where the
  sustained cost lives. Median and max want different fixes here, and one
  bracket now separates them.

  THIS IS EXACTLY WHY THE INSTRUMENT WENT IN FIRST. The subagent that proposed
  the cache said plainly it was a hypothesis from reading rather than a profile,
  and recommended these brackets as the cheaper first move. Had the cache been
  written instead, it would have been measured against another number from the
  same unbracketed bucket and could easily have been reported as a win on noise.

  NEXT: `other` needs subdividing -- pops/lookup, admission+quota, and the
  per-candidate record work are the three obvious splits. Not done tonight.

  SUBDIVIDING `other`, ROUND 1: THE QUEUE PICK IS NOT IT EITHER.
  Leg dispatch-pick, same config:

    dispatchMs=20.50 = airProof 0.31 + band 0.00 + submit 1.83 + pick 0.01 + other 18.35
    dispatchMs=20.74 = airProof 0.30 + band 0.00 + submit 1.61 + pick 0.01 + other 18.82
    dispatchMs=19.67 = airProof 0.27 + band 0.00 + submit 1.31 + pick 0.01 + other 18.08

  The ring-quota scan walks every level's queue head on every iteration and was
  the obvious candidate for a fixed per-candidate cost. It measures 0.01-0.03 ms.
  Not it.

  THE SPECULATIVE HALF IS NOT IT EITHER, and this one needed no new code -- the
  tick-budget line already carries the sub-total:
    "specEnum=0.1 specDispatch=0.0 specPark=0.0 (of dispatch)"
  against dispatch=1985.1 per 5 s window. Zero. Checked before bracketing
  anything, because a counter that already exists is cheaper than one that does
  not -- and this is the second time tonight an existing sub-total answered a
  question I was about to write code for.

  SO `other` IS ~18-19 ms SITTING BETWEEN THE RECORD LOOKUP AND THE SUBMIT.
  Next suspect, now bracketed and awaiting a leg: NeedsOverlayAwarePath, which
  the file's own comment describes as "AT LEVEL 0 ... exactly
  ChunkHasEditedBrick(Key) -- an overlay scan of the chunk plus one brick of
  border on every axis". A scan per candidate is precisely the shape of a fixed
  cost that does not vary with tracked count.

  MY OWN HARNESS MISTAKE, RECORDED BECAUSE THE STAMP IS A SAFETY DEVICE.
  I ran tools/voxel-stamp-build.ps1 in the same command as a build that FAILED
  (LNK1104 -- a leg's editor still held the DLL). The stamp is written only
  after a build that succeeded, precisely so a later leg cannot run against a
  tree that was never compiled; stamping unconditionally destroys that.

  No measurement was harmed: the failed build was C++ only, no .usf changed
  since the last good build, and the missing bracket would have shown up as an
  ABSENT field in the log line rather than a wrong number. But the stamp was
  re-taken after a successful build before any further leg. The rule is
  "&& stamp", never "; stamp".

  SUBDIVIDING `other`, ROUND 2: THE OVERLAY SCAN IS NOT IT EITHER.
  Leg dispatch-overlay:

    dispatchMs=20.15 = airProof 0.27 + band 0.00 + submit 1.44 + pick 0.01
                       + overlay 0.00 + other 18.43
    dispatchMs=21.12 = airProof 0.46 + band 0.00 + submit 2.08 + pick 0.01
                       + overlay 0.00 + other 18.56

  NeedsOverlayAwarePath measures 0.00 ms. So does the band. So does the pick.
  FIVE BRACKETS INSIDE THE PER-CANDIDATE LOOP ACCOUNT FOR ABOUT 2 ms OF A 21 ms
  DISPATCH, and every named suspect is dead:

    IsChunkProvablyAllAir (the recommended optimisation)   ~0.3 ms   2%
    NeedsOverlayAwarePath / ChunkHasEditedBrick             0.00 ms
    the footprint-band consult                              0.00 ms
    the ring-quota scan and the queue pop                   0.02 ms
    building and submitting the job                        ~1.7 ms   8%
    the speculative half (existing sub-total)               0.0 ms

  THAT PATTERN IS ITSELF THE FINDING: if the loop body were the cost, at least
  one of five brackets covering its named calls would have shown it. The cost is
  very probably NOT IN THE LOOP BODY AT ALL.

  SO THE NEXT INSTRUMENT IS A BISECTOR, NOT ANOTHER SUSPECT. One bracket around
  the entire `while (CpuJobsOutstanding() < MaxJobsInFlight)` loop: loopMs
  against dispatchMs answers, in a single leg, whether to keep subdividing the
  loop or to go look at what DispatchJobs does around it. Guessing at a sixth
  suspect would have been the third guess in a row, and the first two were both
  wrong.

================================================================================
2026-08-22  THE BISECTOR ANSWERED IT: `dispatchMs` IS NOT DISPATCH.
================================================================================

Leg dispatch-bisect:

  dispatchMs=20.47 ... loopMs=1.83
  dispatchMs=24.72 ... loopMs=2.54
  dispatchMs=20.45 ... loopMs=1.95
  dispatchMs=20.90 ... loopMs=2.01

THE ENTIRE PER-CANDIDATE LOOP IS 0.9-2.7 ms OF A ~21 ms BUCKET -- about 10%.
Ninety per cent of what this project calls "dispatch" happens OUTSIDE the loop
that dispatches.

AND THE REASON IS THAT THE BUCKET IS MISLABELLED. ThisFrameDispatchMs is
(T1 - T0) + (T3b - T3), and T0..T1 spans:

    DispatchJobs();
    if (GpuMeshJobs.IsValid()) { GpuMeshJobs->Tick(); }   <-- ALSO IN THE BUCKET
    T1 = FPlatformTime::Seconds();

GpuMeshJobs->Tick() is FVoxelGpuMeshJobManager::Tick -- it promotes queued jobs,
BUILDS ONE RDG GRAPH FOR THE WHOLE BATCH, polls readbacks, publishes bricks into
the pool and runs its own brick Flush. That is not dispatch in any sense the
name suggests, and the placement is deliberate and correct for ORDERING reasons
(the comment above it explains why it must sit between dispatch and drain) --
it was simply never carved out of the timing.

WHY THIS MATTERS MORE THAN THE NUMBER. Every conclusion drawn from
"dispatch=1985 ms per 5 s window, ~0.21 ms per dispatched chunk" -- including
the subagent's cache proposal, and my own reading of it earlier tonight -- was
reasoning about a bucket whose contents nobody had checked. The per-chunk figure
was real; the attribution was not. THE FIX BEING SIZED WAS FOR THE WRONG
FUNCTION.

Bracket added around GpuMeshJobs->Tick() to measure the split directly rather
than infer it from the residue. If gpuMgrTickMs lands near 18 ms, the streaming
tick's largest item is the GPU mesh job manager, and the throughput ceiling is a
GPU-pipeline question, not a candidate-loop one.

FIVE SUSPECTS DIED TO GET HERE and every one was a reasonable guess from
reading: the uncached worldgen bound, the overlay scan, the band consult, the
ring-quota scan, the speculative half. The bisector -- one bracket around the
whole loop, asking "is it even in here" -- was worth more than all five, and it
should have been the first instrument rather than the sixth.

  MEASURED, AND THE SUM CLOSES EXACTLY. Leg dispatch-gpumgr:

    dispatchMs=19.28 | loopMs=0.47 gpuMgrTickMs=18.81   (0.47 + 18.81 = 19.28)
    dispatchMs=22.35 | loopMs=3.33 gpuMgrTickMs=19.02   (3.33 + 19.02 = 22.35)
    dispatchMs=20.41 | loopMs=2.20 gpuMgrTickMs=18.21   (2.20 + 18.21 = 20.41)
    dispatchMs=20.71 | loopMs=2.08 gpuMgrTickMs=18.63   (2.08 + 18.63 = 20.71)

  loopMs + gpuMgrTickMs = dispatchMs, to the hundredth, on every sampled frame.
  Nothing is left over, which is the strongest validation the instrumentation
  could have: the bucket is fully accounted for by exactly two things, and one
  of them is 90% of it.

  ============================================================================
  THE ANSWER: THE STREAMING TICK'S LARGEST ITEM IS
  FVoxelGpuMeshJobManager::Tick(), AT ~18-19 ms PER HITCH FRAME.
  ============================================================================

  Not the dispatch loop (0.5-3.3 ms). Not the worldgen bound. Not the overlay
  scan, the band, the quota scan or the speculative half. The GPU mesh job
  manager's tick -- which promotes queued jobs, builds ONE RDG GRAPH FOR THE
  WHOLE BATCH, polls two readback phases, publishes bricks into the pool and
  runs its own brick Flush.

  WHAT THIS REDIRECTS. Throughput is MaxJobsInFlight x frame rate, and the frame
  rate is set by the streaming tick, whose largest item is now named. Every
  candidate fix discussed tonight -- memoising FootprintSurfaceUpperBoundMm,
  batching the submit, trimming the quota scan -- targets the 10%. The 90% is a
  GPU-PIPELINE question: graph construction cost per tick, readback polling, and
  how much of it is proportional to jobs promoted rather than to jobs in flight.

  Note this is also where brick publication happens, which connects it to the
  brickFlush figure already on the tick-budget line (1,363-1,510 ms per 5 s
  window, 32-41% of the tick) -- the two may be measuring overlapping work, and
  that is the first thing to check before sizing anything.

  NOT OPTIMISED TONIGHT, DELIBERATELY. The whole point of the last six brackets
  is that this project has repeatedly sized fixes against buckets nobody had
  opened. The next step is to bracket inside FVoxelGpuMeshJobManager::Tick --
  promote, graph build, readback poll, publish -- and only then choose.

================================================================================
2026-08-22  BRACKETING INSIDE FVoxelGpuMeshJobManager::Tick -- the 90%
================================================================================

Four stages, read-and-reset through GetAndResetTickStageMs() so the caller owns
the window and two readers cannot halve each other's totals:

  promoteMs     selecting jobs into the batch (the while loop against MaxInFlight)
  enqueueMs     building and handing over the render command -- A SUBSET OF
                promoteMs, because DispatchBatch is called from inside that
                stage. Promote-proper is promoteMs - enqueueMs, and that is
                stated at BOTH the source and the log site: a breakdown whose
                parts silently overlap is worse than one number.
  pollMs        PollInFlight -- harvesting the two readback phases
  brickFlushMs  the GetGlobalVoxelBrickPool().Flush() this tick owns

WHY enqueueMs IS SEPARATED AT ALL. The ENQUEUE_RENDER_COMMAND lambda runs on the
RENDER thread; what the game thread pays is constructing the command and handing
it over. If that number is large it means the enqueue is BLOCKING, which is a
different and far more serious finding than a slow graph -- and the two are
indistinguishable in the parent's single number.

A SCOPE BUG I MADE AND CAUGHT AT THE COMPILER. My first attempt closed the
enqueue bracket against a variable declared in Tick(), from inside DispatchBatch
-- a different function entirely. Tick() ends at :1046 and DispatchBatch begins
at :1048; the two read as one block when skimming. It failed to compile, which
is the right outcome, and it is the same lesson as the VOXEL_MARCH_HAS_BRICKPOOL
guard earlier tonight: on this codebase the loud failures are the lucky ones.

Prediction to be checked against the leg, written down BEFORE reading it: pollMs
is the likeliest home for 18 ms, because readback harvesting is the one stage
that can wait on the GPU. If instead promoteMs dominates, the cost is job
selection and the fix is a different shape entirely. If enqueueMs dominates, the
render-command handoff is blocking and that is the most serious of the three.

  RESULT, AND MY PRE-REGISTERED PREDICTION WAS WRONG. Leg gpumgr-stages:

    Voxel GPU mesh tick (5s window): promoteMs=0.6 (of which enqueueMs=0.2)
                                     pollMs=1.4  brickFlushMs=1170.8
    ... promoteMs=0.6 enqueueMs=0.2 pollMs=1.4 brickFlushMs=1860.6
    ... promoteMs=0.6 enqueueMs=0.2 pollMs=1.4 brickFlushMs=1813.5

  I predicted pollMs, on the reasoning that readback harvesting is the one stage
  that can wait on the GPU. It is 1.4 ms per 5 s WINDOW. Job promotion is 0.6.
  The render-command enqueue is 0.2 and therefore not blocking, which retires
  the most serious of the three possibilities.

  ============================================================================
  IT IS FVoxelBrickPool::Flush(). 1,170-1,860 ms PER 5 s WINDOW, INSIDE THE GPU
  MANAGER'S TICK ALONE -- roughly 18 ms per tick, which is the whole of the
  ~18-19 ms this stage costs.
  ============================================================================

  AND THAT IS ONLY ONE OF ITS TWO CALL SITES. The tick-budget line's separate
  `brickFlush` bucket is the WORLD SUBSYSTEM's own Flush, and it reads
  1,324-1,686 ms per 5 s window in the same legs. Both do real work: the GPU
  manager flushes, then DrainResults publishes CPU-arm bricks, then the
  subsystem flushes again.

    GPU manager Flush   ~1.2-1.9 s per 5 s window   (inside `dispatch`)
    subsystem Flush     ~1.3-1.7 s per 5 s window   (the `brickFlush` bucket)
    ---------------------------------------------------------------
    ~2.5-3.5 s of a ~3.7-4.0 s STREAMING TICK IS FLUSHING THE BRICK POOL.

  That is the throughput ceiling, and it explains every earlier reading at once:
  why the tick is 73-84% of wall clock, why `dispatch` looked enormous while its
  loop measured 2 ms, and why five plausible micro-optimisations in that loop
  were each worth ~0%.

  I DID NOT CAUSE IT, BUT I SHOULD CHECK WHETHER I MADE IT WORSE. brickFlush
  reads 1,324.7-1,516.2 ms per 5 s on water-cost-control, a MOVING leg taken
  BEFORE Wave 1.2 was built, against 1,683.6-1,685.9 now. So the dominant cost
  predates brick removal; Wave 1.2 plausibly adds 10-25% by queueing clears, but
  the poses and flights differ and that comparison is not clean enough to quote.
  A same-pose A/B is the honest way to settle it and has not been run.

  ALSO RETIRED: the note in this file at :2214 -- "GAME THREAD IS NOT THE COST:
  brickFlush=0.3-0.4 ms per 5 s window". True when written, on a STATIC cold-fill
  leg. Under motion it is three to four THOUSAND times larger. A measurement's
  conditions are part of the measurement.

================================================================================
2026-08-22  SPLITTING Flush() ITSELF -- prep vs enqueue vs sink
================================================================================

Flush() is ~18 ms per tick and dominates the streaming tick, but reading it
shows the game-thread work BEFORE the render command is two MoveTemps, a
Reserve and a loop building the index delta. That does not obviously cost 18 ms,
which raises a specific alternative:

  THE ENQUEUE ITSELF MAY BE BLOCKING ON RENDER-THREAD BACKPRESSURE.

If so, `brickFlush` is not measuring flush work at all -- it is measuring the
GAME THREAD WAITING FOR THE RENDER THREAD, and every "make the flush cheaper"
idea would move nothing. The two have opposite fixes and the single number
cannot separate them, which is the same shape as `dispatch` an hour ago.

Three brackets, summed across BOTH call sites because the pool owns the counter:
  prepMs     building the index delta, moving the pending queues
  enqueueMs  ENQUEUE_RENDER_COMMAND -- the suspect
  sinkMs     handing the delta to the march chunk index

HOW TO READ IT, WRITTEN BEFORE THE LEG LANDS:
  enqueue dominates  -> render-thread backpressure. The fix is upstream, in what
                        the render thread is doing, and NOT in Flush. It would
                        also mean the game thread is not really the bottleneck --
                        it is blocked BY the bottleneck, which reframes the whole
                        "game-thread bound" conclusion.
  prep dominates     -> genuine game-thread work; the index delta construction is
                        the only thing there big enough to matter.
  sink dominates     -> FVoxelMarchChunkIndex::ApplyDelta, which is mine (Wave
                        1.2 made Removed non-empty for the first time) and would
                        be the one result tonight where I made the ceiling worse.

That third outcome is the one I most want to be wrong about, which is exactly
why it is written down before the number arrives.

  RESULT: IT IS THE SINK, AND THAT IS THE OUTCOME I FLAGGED AS THE ONE I MOST
  WANTED TO BE WRONG ABOUT.

    Voxel brick flush (5s window, both call sites):
      prepMs=0.1  enqueueMs=0.3  sinkMs=2647.6
      prepMs=0.1  enqueueMs=0.4  sinkMs=3437.7
      prepMs=0.1  enqueueMs=0.4  sinkMs=3465.2

  The enqueue is 0.3-0.4 ms per 5 s WINDOW, so render-thread backpressure is
  retired outright -- the game thread is not waiting on anything here. The prep
  is 0.1. THE ENTIRE COST OF FLUSHING THE BRICK POOL IS IndexSink(IndexDelta),
  i.e. FVoxelMarchChunkIndex::ApplyDelta, at 2.6-3.5 SECONDS PER 5 SECOND WINDOW
  across both call sites.

  So the chain is now fully resolved, four levels down:
    streaming tick (73-84% of wall)
      -> `dispatch` bucket (~44%), which is 90% NOT dispatch
        -> FVoxelGpuMeshJobManager::Tick
          -> FVoxelBrickPool::Flush
            -> FVoxelMarchChunkIndex::ApplyDelta        <-- THE CEILING

  AND I MAY HAVE CAUSED A LARGE PART OF IT. `Delta.Removed` was ALWAYS EMPTY
  until Wave 1.2 landed brick removal this morning -- that is stated in
  VoxelBrickPool.h's own header ("Both halves of Removed below are therefore
  always empty today"). So the Removed loop is new work that did not exist
  yesterday. It is equally possible the cost is the Added loop or
  MarkDirtyAndUpload, both of which predate the wave.

  BRACKETING ALL THREE rather than reasoning about it: removedMs (n),
  addedMs (n), uploadMs. MarkDirtyAndUpload is separated because it is paid ONCE
  PER FLUSH regardless of how many entries moved -- so if it dominates, the fix
  is flush frequency or an incremental upload and has nothing to do with either
  loop. The plan already warned about exactly this call ("every removal costs a
  full grid rebuild-and-hash on the game thread in MarkDirtyAndUpload"), which
  makes it the leading candidate and therefore the one most worth measuring
  rather than assuming.

  A COUNTER THAT READ ZERO AND MEASURED NOTHING -- CAUGHT BEFORE IT WAS QUOTED.

  The first applyDelta leg reported addedMs=0.0 (n=0) and uploadMs=0.0 on every
  window, alongside sinkMs=2,413-3,219 ms. I nearly wrote that up as "Added is
  empty, so Wave 1.2's Removed loop is the whole cost".

  IT WAS NOT A MEASUREMENT. A scripted edit had asserted and died before writing
  the Added and Upload brackets, so those two fields were never incremented
  anywhere -- they were default-initialised zeros printed with a plausible label.
  Only the Removed bracket had landed. `grep -n "ApplyDeltaMs\."` showed two
  lines where there should have been five.

  WHAT GAVE IT AWAY WAS ARITHMETIC, NOT SUSPICION: sinkMs 3,219 ms against
  ApplyDelta's own brackets summing to 5.6 ms is 0.2% accounted for. When the
  earlier dispatch split closed to the hundredth, this one did not close at all,
  and a breakdown that does not close is a broken instrument until proven
  otherwise. THE HABIT WORTH KEEPING: always print the residual, and always
  check it sums.

  This is the exact failure class this file documents throughout -- the
  weathering pass that removed 20 voxels of 90,000, the fork that carried zero
  traffic, the pre-ROP counters that looked healthy on a blank frame. It very
  nearly produced a wrong headline about my own change.

  Brackets now verified present at :492-493, :532-533 and :541 before the leg was
  run, rather than trusted from a script's exit message.

================================================================================
2026-08-22  THE CEILING, FOUND. AND IT IS A HASH FOR A DISABLED DIAGNOSTIC.
================================================================================

Leg applydelta-full, with all three brackets verified present before running:

  applyDelta (5s window): removedMs=0.0 (n=0)  addedMs=2.0 (n=9570) uploadMs=3190.0
  applyDelta (5s window): removedMs=0.0 (n=0)  addedMs=1.4 (n=9200) uploadMs=3167.9
  applyDelta (5s window): removedMs=0.0 (n=0)  addedMs=1.4 (n=9099) uploadMs=3146.5
  brick flush  sinkMs=3192.1 / 3169.5 / 3148.1

  uploadMs + addedMs = sinkMs, to the tenth, on every window. The breakdown
  closes, which is what makes it a measurement rather than a guess.

MarkDirtyAndUpload IS 3,146-3,190 ms PER 5 SECOND WINDOW, against a streaming
tick totalling ~3,700 ms. IT IS ~85% OF THE TICK. And since throughput is
MaxJobsInFlight x frame rate, and the frame rate is set by the tick, IT IS THE
WORLD'S GENERATION CEILING.

WHAT IT IS DOING: an FNV-1a hash over the WHOLE 4 MiB chunk-index grid --
1,048,576 iterations -- on the GAME THREAD, once per flush, unconditionally.
About 180 flushes per 5 s window across the two call sites, so ~190 MILLION
hash steps per window.

ITS ONLY CONSUMER IS voxel.March.VerifySource. One reader, at
VoxelMarchRenderer.cpp:4471, feeding MarchIndexHash into the source comparator.
That cvar DEFAULTS TO 0. And even when set, the comparator never arms today,
because its settle criterion ANDs a term counted in render frames with one
counted in 5-second perf ticks (recorded earlier tonight).

  So the single most expensive thing in the streaming tick exists to feed a
  diagnostic that is off by default and cannot currently run.

AND WAVE 1.2 IS EXONERATED, WHICH I WOULD HAVE GOT WRONG WITHOUT THIS SPLIT.
removedMs is 0.0 with n=0 in these windows, and 5.6 ms with n=26,553 at its
busiest earlier. Brick removal is not the cost and never was. I had written down
that "Removed was always empty until Wave 1.2" as a reason to suspect my own
change; the measurement says the cost is a hash that predates it entirely and is
paid once per flush regardless of what moved.

FIX: gate the hash on its consumer. FVoxelMarchChunkIndex::SetContentHashEnabled,
default OFF, switched on by the comparator at the point it reads the value. The
index keeps no opinion about who wants it, and the diagnostic keeps working when
it is actually asked for.

GATE FOR THE VERIFYING LEG: uploadMs must collapse to ~0, sinkMs with it, the
tick budget's dispatch and brickFlush buckets must both fall by seconds, and
brickPacks/s must RISE -- because the game thread is what was capping it.

  GATE RESULT: THE STREAMING TICK COLLAPSED. Leg hashgate-verify against
  applydelta-full (same flight, same config, hash gated off):

    uploadMs      3,146-3,190  ->  155-880 ms per 5 s window
    brickFlush    1,627-1,806  ->  108-407 ms
    dispatch      1,985-2,311  ->  670-680 ms
    tickMs        3,564-4,223  ->  930-1,602 ms
    % of wall     71-84%       ->  18-32%

  THE STREAMING TICK WENT FROM ~75-85% OF WALL CLOCK TO ~20-30%. That is the
  single largest change measured on this project, and it came from deleting work
  rather than optimising it.

  uploadMs does NOT go to zero (155-880 ms remains), and it should not: the hash
  was only part of MarkDirtyAndUpload. What is left is the actual grid upload
  path, which is real work with a real consumer. The hash was the removable half.

  THROUGHPUT IS NOT YET CLEARLY BETTER, AND I AM NOT CLAIMING IT IS.
  brickPacks/s reads 1,301 / 1,953 / 2,194 after against 1,743 / 1,756 / 2,001
  before -- overlapping ranges on a noisy metric. Throughput is
  MaxJobsInFlight x frame rate, so freeing the game thread should raise it, and
  the fact that it has not clearly done so means SOMETHING ELSE IS NOW BINDING.
  That is the expected outcome of removing a bottleneck and it is the next
  question, not a disappointment. The pre-registered gate said "brickPacks/s must
  RISE"; on this evidence it has not, and that is recorded as stated rather than
  reinterpreted.

  What IS unambiguous: the game thread is no longer the constraint it was, and
  ~2.5 seconds per 5 seconds of game-thread time has been returned.

  CORRECTION TO MY OWN "THROUGHPUT DID NOT RISE" NOTE ABOVE. That was written
  from mid-flight windows before the leg finished. The steady-state windows:

    brickPacks/s   1,743 / 1,756 / 2,001   ->   2,274 / 2,406 / 2,435 / 2,430
    tickMs         3,564-4,223 (71-84%)    ->   1,804-2,118 (36-42%)

  THROUGHPUT IS UP ROUGHLY 25%, and the pre-registered gate ("brickPacks/s must
  RISE") is met. I should have waited for the leg rather than reading a partial
  series -- the fifth unlike-for-unlike comparison of the night, and the same
  windowing error as the retention holes.

  BUT p50 FRAME TIME WENT UP, AND THAT IS NOT HIDDEN:
    applydelta-full  frames=7542  p50=8.01 ms  p95=63.78  max=313.35
    hashgate-verify  frames=8199  p50=15.27 ms p95=72.83  max=301.62

  More chunks per second AND a longer median frame. The consistent reading is
  that the game thread was previously spending its time on the hash and NOT
  generating, so frames were short because little was happening; with the hash
  gone it does 25% more real streaming work per second and each frame carries
  more of it. That is a plausible story and it is NOT yet a measured one -- the
  honest statement is that the tick's SHARE fell by half while its OUTPUT rose,
  and the frame-time move needs its own attribution before anyone calls this a
  pure win.

  DO NOT SHIP THIS AS "25% faster streaming" WITHOUT THAT. Two numbers moved in
  opposite directions and only one of them is the one people feel.

================================================================================
2026-08-22  A UNITY-BUILD COLLISION THAT ADAPTIVE UNITY HAD BEEN HIDING
================================================================================

A build failed with:
  VoxelDetailAssetSubsystem.cpp(252): error C2374: 'kCoverPitchMm': redefinition
  VoxelDetailAssetSubsystem.cpp(437): error C2371: 'kGroupEdgeVoxels': redefinition

VoxelCoverVerify.cpp and VoxelDetailAssetSubsystem.cpp BOTH define kCoverPitchMm
and kGroupEdgeVoxels in their anonymous namespaces. Separate translation units
make that legal; a unity build concatenates them into one where it is a
redefinition.

IT WAS NOT MY CHANGE, AND THE REASON IT SURFACED IS THE INTERESTING PART. UBT's
ADAPTIVE UNITY excludes recently-edited files from the unity blob -- every build
tonight printed "[Adaptive Build] Excluded from VoxelEarth unity file: ...
VoxelCoverVerify.cpp, VoxelDetailAssetSubsystem.cpp ...". Both colliding files
had been edited recently enough to be excluded on every build that touched them.
The collision appeared the moment one aged back in.

SO THE HAZARD IS: A LATENT UNITY COLLISION IS INVISIBLE FOR EXACTLY AS LONG AS
SOMEONE KEEPS EDITING THE FILES. It surfaces later, on someone else's build, in
a file they did not touch.

tools/lint-unity-collisions.py exists for this and named one of the two pairs
(kGroupEdgeVoxels) before the fix; it did not name kCoverPitchMm, so the linter
has a blind spot worth a look. Fixed by renaming the VERIFY file's copies to
kCoverVerifyPitchMm / kCoverVerifyGroupEdgeVoxels -- the harness is the less
central of the two, and the linter's prescribed alternative (per-file NAMED
namespaces) would have been a larger edit to code unrelated to this work.
Linter now reports "clean (65 .cpp files in 2 module(s))".

RUNNING THE LINTER SHOULD PRECEDE A BUILD AFTER ANY SESSION THAT EDITS SEVERAL
FILES IN ONE MODULE, because that is exactly the state in which adaptive unity
is hiding the most.

================================================================================
2026-08-22  voxel.March.IndexContentHash -- the A/B switch the last one needed
================================================================================

The 4 MiB index hash is now forceable from a cvar (default 0), so its cost can be
measured IN ONE BINARY. The before/after that produced the headline spanned two
builds, and the first thing that comparison produced was a p50 move
(8.01 -> 15.27 ms) that nothing could attribute -- the two-legs-two-binaries
problem this project keeps paying for.

Applied on the GAME THREAD in VoxelMarchEnsureExtension, not from the render
thread where the comparator enables it: MarkDirtyAndUpload reads the flag from
the game thread during a flush, and a flag written by one thread and read by the
other every flush is a race on a value that decides whether ~85% of the
streaming tick happens.

The A/B pair (p50ab-hashON against hashgate-verify) is what settles whether the
p50 rise is the hash's absence or something else. Same binary, same flight, same
pose, one cvar apart.

================================================================================
2026-08-22  THE p50 RISE WAS AN ARTIFACT. MY EXPLANATION WAS WRONG.
================================================================================

I wrote that p50 8.01 -> 15.27 ms was probably "the game thread previously
hashing rather than generating, now carrying 25% more real work per frame".
BOTH HALVES ARE REFUTED. Per frame the AFTER leg does slightly LESS work
(103.79 vs 107.59 brickPacks/frame) and its tick is 26.91 ms CHEAPER. The 25%
throughput gain is entirely FRAME RATE, not per-frame work.

WHAT ACTUALLY HAPPENED: the run is BIMODAL and p50 crossed the seam.

The harness runs 90 s preflight + 150 s flight. The preflight cold-fills for
~50 s and then GOES COMPLETELY IDLE for 30 s at the spawn pose -- windows
reading tickMs=13.2 (0.26% of wall), brickPacks=0, at 663-668 frames per 5 s
(133 fps). That idle stretch is about HALF of all post-warmup frames.

  fast (idle) lobe share   BEFORE 3905/7542 = 51.78%  -> median lands INSIDE it
                           AFTER  3973/8199 = 48.46%  -> median lands 1.54 pts PAST it
  and the lobes are 7 ms and 20 ms.

The idle lobe is the same ABSOLUTE size in both legs (3905 vs 3973) -- the
change does not touch the idle path, and the 7.51 / 7.52 ms idle means are the
control that proves it. Its SHARE fell only because the flight got faster and
produced 546 more frames. A 3.3-point shift in lobe fraction stepped the median
across a 13 ms discontinuity.

  segment            BEFORE frames/mean      AFTER frames/mean
  cold fill  w3-12     723 / 69.16 ms          733 / 68.21 ms     unchanged
  IDLE      w13-17    3328 /  7.51 ms         3325 /  7.52 ms     unchanged (control)
  FLIGHT    w19-49    2860 / 54.20 ms         3406 / 45.51 ms     -16.0%

EVERY SEGMENT IS UNCHANGED OR BETTER. p50 measured which lobe the 50th
percentile landed in and nothing else.

THE DEFENSIBLE HEADLINE, replacing everything I wrote last night:
  flight-segment mean frame time  54.20 -> 45.51 ms   (-16%)
  throughput                      1,985 -> 2,281 brickPacks/s  (+15%)
  with idle (7.51/7.52) and cold fill (69.2/68.2) as unchanged controls.

AND TWO CORRECTIONS TO MY OWN NUMBERS:
  * "~85% of the streaming tick" is OVERSTATED. Measured against the flight
    segment the hash was 27.57 of 45.34 ms per frame -- about 61% of tickMs.
    The 85% came from comparing a 5 s window total against a tick total that
    included windows of different character.
  * The sub-stage timers do NOT cleanly partition the saving: uploadMs is
    35.59 ms/frame, larger than any single sub-stage, and the drop splits across
    `dispatch` (-14.0) and `brickFlush` (-13.4) because there are TWO
    Pool.Flush() sites with separate timers. Do not read those sub-stages as a
    partition of the whole.

THE RENDER THREAD IS NOT THE CONSTRAINT: renderMs 24.80 -> 16.00,
renderWaitMs 9.23 -> 1.32 on flight hitch frames. Only gameWaitMs rose
(4.42 -> 8.25).

DO NOT COMPARE THE HITCH-FRAME MEANS (54.53 -> 64.24, which looks like a
regression). The populations are not the same: BEFORE, 98.6% of flight frames
exceeded the 33.3 ms threshold; AFTER, 56.3%. BEFORE's "hitches" are nearly all
frames; AFTER's are a selected worst-half. Sixth instance of the unlike-things
failure, and it was waiting in the very table I would have quoted next.

WHAT THE FLIGHT DISTRIBUTION BECAME: the change CREATED a fast population
(43.7% of flight frames at ~21.4 ms) and left the remaining 56.3% heavier
(~64.2 ms). That is the honest reading of p95 rising 63.78 -> 72.83, and it
lines up with the next item.

NEW, AND THE LARGEST OPEN ITEM NOW: game-thread time OUTSIDE the voxel tick rose
8.85 -> 27.08 ms/frame over the flight (+206%), concentrated on 1,880 hitch
frames at 46.87 ms each. The engine's own counters cannot account for it --
renderMs ~8 + rhiMs ~4 + gameWait ~0 do not close a 45 ms frame. Note
`elsewhereMs` is a RESIDUAL (FMath::Max(0, FrameMs - TickMsSoFar),
VoxelWorldSubsystem.cpp:6086), i.e. "unaccounted", not "measured", and
GRenderThreadTime/GGameThreadWaitTime describe the PREVIOUS frame by the code's
own comment. This needs an instrument, not another leg.

HARNESS CHANGE WORTH MAKING: retire whole-run p50 for this harness or report it
per segment. With a 30 s idle stretch that is ~50% of post-warmup frames, every
percentile below p60 measures the idle scene rather than the workload.

================================================================================
2026-08-22  THE SAME-BINARY A/B. This is the number to quote.
================================================================================

voxel.March.IndexContentHash 1 vs 0, ONE BINARY, same flight, same pose, same
config, one cvar apart. Segment-analysed, because whole-run percentiles on this
harness measure the idle lobe (see the p50 artifact entry above).

                       hash ON        hash OFF
  idle frames          3,331          3,325       <- control, unchanged
  FLIGHT frames        3,089          3,406
  FLIGHT mean frame    50.18 ms       45.51 ms    -9.3%
  tick % of wall       93.5%          40.5%
  uploadMs             3,742-4,224    155-880

  WHOLE-RUN percentiles, recorded ONLY to show they are unusable here:
    hash ON   p50=7.87  p95=59.65  max=306.72
    hash OFF  p50=15.27 p95=72.83  max=301.62
  Read naively that says the hash makes everything faster. It does not; the
  medians sit on opposite sides of the bimodal seam. This pair is the cleanest
  possible demonstration that p50/p95 must not be quoted for this harness.

THE HONEST HEADLINE IS -9.3% ON FLIGHT FRAME TIME, NOT -16%.

The -16% came from comparing across TWO BUILDS (applydelta-full vs
hashgate-verify), which differed by more than the hash -- the earlier build also
carried a different set of instrumentation brackets. The same-binary A/B is
worth more than the cross-build one and it is smaller. Both are real; only one
is attributable.

TICK SHARE IS THE MORE STRIKING HALF: 93.5% -> 40.5% of wall clock. The game
thread was almost entirely consumed by the streaming tick during flight and now
is not. That the frame time only fell 9.3% is the interesting residue -- it says
the freed game-thread time is NOT what was capping the frame, which lines up
exactly with the +18 ms/frame of unattributed non-tick game-thread time the p50
investigation surfaced. The two findings agree: something outside the voxel tick
is holding the flight frame, and it is now the largest item in the project.

================================================================================
2026-08-22  THE FLIP HAS A SECOND SILENT VISUAL REGRESSION: VOXEL GI
================================================================================

VERIFIED BY GREP, not inferred:
  * VoxelQuadVertexFactory.ush is the ONLY shader in the tree that reads
    `VoxelGIVol.` -- one file, confirmed by `grep -l "VoxelGIVol\." Shaders/*`.
  * VoxelQuadVertexFactory.cpp:225 is the only site that binds the GI uniform
    buffer for RENDERING. (VoxelGIMarchPass.cpp:188 registers the volumes for
    COMPUTE -- that is the producer, not a consumer.)
  * VoxelMarch.usf:1069 writes EncodeIndirectIrradiance(1.0f) -- the NEUTRAL
    value. The marcher does not sample GI at all.
  * voxel.GI.Enabled DEFAULTS TO 1 (VoxelGI.cpp:66-67).
  * The GI block in the vertex factory is NOT gated on WaterMode.

SO THE MOMENT TERRAIN LEAVES THE QUAD PATH, TERRAIN LOSES VOXEL GI ENTIRELY AND
SILENTLY. This is the same shape as the shadow finding earlier tonight and it
was hiding in the same place: a feature the quad path provides, that the
marcher's frame-time advantage is partly funded by not providing, with no error
and no counter.

TWO SILENT VISUAL REGRESSIONS NOW GATE THE FLIP, NOT ONE:
  1. sun shadows      -- measured: 42.0M primitives / 15.8 ms on the quad path,
                         ZERO in the marcher arm. S2 marched shadows now exist
                         (0.335-0.409 ms) but reach only 64 m.
  2. voxel GI         -- terrain gets none under the marcher. Nothing measured
                         yet; needs a lit-cave A/B.

THE GI ORIGIN IS ALSO ALREADY WRONG FOR WATER, WHICH REFRAMES "REPLACE THE
ANCHOR". FVoxelGIVolume is a TGlobalResource -- ONE instance -- and its
OriginPoolUU is computed from the TERRAIN pool's component location
(VoxelGI.cpp:1264, 1381). That single uniform is bound identically to EVERY
FVoxelQuadVertexFactory draw, including all of water's buckets, each of which
carries its own unrelated WaterPoolBucketRebase. There is no per-primitive
origin term in either uniform struct. So water's GI lookup is offset by
(WaterBucketRebase - GpuPoolRebase) -- kilometres -- and bInsideVolume is false
essentially always, i.e. WATER SILENTLY RECEIVES NO GI TODAY.

The header states the assumption that makes this a bug rather than an oversight:
"Pool space is the only correct space for this... THE POOL COMPONENT carries the
big offset" (VoxelGIVolume.h:69-74, singular). True when written -- before W5
split water into buckets.

CONSEQUENCE FOR THE PLAN: item "replace the GI volume anchor" is NOT "find a
terrain origin so the empty stand-in pool can be deleted". It is "make the GI
origin per-consumer, because terrain's pool was never the right space for the
consumer that survives". The empty stand-in is propping up a coordinate system
for WATER, and propping it up incorrectly.

NOT CONFIRMED IN A PICTURE. This is arithmetic over the code plus the absence of
any compensating term. Falsifiable cheaply: log OriginPoolUU beside each pool's
GetComponentLocation() for one frame, or capture a lake with voxel.GI.Enabled
0 vs 1 -- by this reading the pair should be IDENTICAL, which is the falsifier
and the acceptance test in one shot.

ALSO CORRECTED: VoxelGI.cpp:440-450 says "up to FIVE UVoxelGpuPoolComponents in
a live world" and my plan addendum repeats it. Water alone can hold TWELVE
(kMaxWaterPoolBuckets = 12, VoxelWaterSubsystem.cpp:1954). True live maximum is
16 counting the two debug verifiers and the never-registered test.

AND ONE CLAIM OF THE AUDIT'S THAT I CAN RETIRE FROM MEASUREMENT: it lists
"whether terrain currently casts shadows at all" as unresolved, citing
shadowGathers=0. That is the stale comment retired earlier tonight -- terrain
casts, at 42M primitives and 15.8 ms, once -VoxelShadowDistanceM is passed.
